Not every single thing, I’m not saying every conversation in the room is about me, but there were times when things that looked unrelated to other people felt connected to what my in-laws were doing.\n\nLike if something came up at the parish committee, or someone made a comment about the altar, or even if people suddenly got quiet when I walked near them, it didn’t feel separate. It felt like it was all part of the same thing — pushing me out, making me look like I didn’t belong there anymore. Other people might say, “Oh, that was just a meeting,” or “That person was just talking,” but when you are the one it’s happening to, you notice the pattern.\n\nThat is why I came in, really. Because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me like a normal family disagreement. They were using the altar and the parish committee, these respectable things, to push me out little by little and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. So yes, when I hear certain conversations or see certain decisions being made, I do connect them back to that. It’s hard not to, because it all fits together in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is anything your in-laws could do or say that would make you believe they are not plotting against you?\n\nPatient: I mean… they could say it, yes. They could sit there and say, “We’re not plotting against you, we’re not trying to push you out,” but I don’t know that just saying it would make me believe them. Because people can say anything when they know they’re being watched or questioned.\n\nWhat would matter to me is if their actions changed. If they stopped using the altar and the parish committee like that, stopped making decisions around me, stopped acting like my place in the family and in the church doesn’t count. Because that’s why I came in — I knew this wasn’t just some ordinary argument with my in-laws. It felt bigger than that. They were using those church things, the altar, the committee, to push me aside and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nSo if you’re asking if there is one sentence they could say that would make me think, “Oh, I was wrong,” then no, not really. I would need to see something real. I would need them to acknowledge what they did, not just deny it. Because if they only deny it, that just feels like more of the same — making me look like I’m the problem while they keep their hands clean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the plot against you is simple and straightforward or complex and involving many steps?\n\nPatient: It’s not simple. If it was simple, I think maybe people would understand me better, because then I could point to one thing and say, there, that’s what they did. But it’s not like that. It’s… it’s layered.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me, like a normal family disagreement. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, little by little, and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That takes steps. That takes people acting like everything is ordinary, like it’s just church business, when really it changes how others see me and where I’m allowed to stand.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s straightforward. It involves the family, the church, the committee, the way things are said and not said. It’s not just one conversation or one insult. It’s a whole arrangement, and that’s what makes it so hard to explain without sounding like I’m making too much of it. But I’m not. I know what I’ve seen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your in-laws' actions against you might be exaggerated or distorted in any way?\n\nPatient: Exaggerated? I… I know it may sound that way to you, because you’re hearing it from the outside. I understand that. If someone just says, “My in-laws are using church things against me,” people might think, “Well, maybe she’s taking it too personally.” I’m not unaware of how it sounds.\n\nBut to me, it doesn’t feel exaggerated. I came in because I knew this was not just my in-laws having a disagreement with me or being cold to me at family gatherings. It was more than that. They were using the altar, and the parish committee, these places where people are supposed to be respectful and fair, to push me out little by little. And not just push me out of a room — push me out of people’s minds, make everyone forget what I have done for this family.\n\nSo when you ask if it could be distorted, I don’t know how to answer that except to say I don’t think I’m making it up. Maybe I get upset when I talk about it, yes. Maybe I repeat myself because nobody seems to really hear the whole thing. But the pattern is there. It’s not just one comment or one decision. It’s the way they act, the way they use those church roles, the way suddenly my place doesn’t matter anymore.\n\nI suppose anything can look different depending on who is looking at it, but from where I’m standing, no, I don’t think I’m exaggerating. I think I’m finally saying out loud what has been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the plot against you is affecting your daily life in significant ways?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always been the one who could keep things going. The parish preparations, the festival things, the lists, who brings what, what needs to be cleaned, what needs to be set up — I used to be able to hold all of that in my head. And the house too. I had my way of doing things.\n\nBut lately I just… I couldn’t keep up. I would start something and then lose track of it, or I’d sit there thinking about what they were doing and how they were pushing me out, and the whole day would go. The festival tasks had to be taken over by my family because I wasn’t managing them the way I normally would. That was embarrassing, honestly, because those things mattered to me.\n\nAnd at home, it wasn’t just that the house was a little messy. I wasn’t keeping the routines. I was missing meals sometimes, forgetting what I was supposed to do next, not really taking care of myself the way I should. My husband started staying close to me because he could see I wasn’t reliable with those things. Not because I’m helpless, but because this has taken over so much space in my mind that ordinary things started slipping. So yes, it’s affecting my daily life a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any evidence or information that could be presented to you that would make you question or change your beliefs about your in-laws plotting against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know what evidence would change it, because the evidence is sort of already in how it happened. It wasn’t one thing where someone could hand me a paper and say, “See, this proves it,” or, “This disproves it.” That’s part of why it’s so hard to talk about.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me. If it were just an argument, I could accept that maybe tempers got high or people said things they didn’t mean. But this was them using the altar, using the parish committee, using those positions and those little decisions to move me aside. And then people act like it’s nothing, like it’s just normal church business, but it changes everything. It changes where I stand, what people remember, how they look at me, whether they acknowledge what I’ve done for this family.\n\nSo if you ask me, “Could someone show you something to make you question it?” I suppose if they all came forward honestly and admitted what they were doing, or if the committee stopped being used that way, maybe I could think differently. But just someone telling me, “No, that’s not happening,” wouldn’t be enough. Because I’ve already seen the pattern. I’ve seen the way I’m pushed out and then expected to smile like I don’t notice.\n\nI’m not trying to be stubborn. I know you’re asking whether I can consider another explanation. I can hear the question. But I don’t think this is a misunderstanding. I think people want me to doubt myself because then they don’t have to answer for what they’ve done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that when people talk about you or your situation, they are always referring to something specific that you've done or something about you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, a lot of the time it is about me, even when they don’t say my name directly. That’s what makes it so hard to explain, because people can pretend it’s just a general comment, or just church business, or just a committee decision. But I know the difference between something ordinary and something that is meant to point at me.\n\nWhen they talk about who should be involved, who should stand where, who should be recognized, who has “done enough” or who is “causing tension,” I hear that and I know it’s not just floating in the air. It goes back to what I’ve done, what I’ve tried to do for this family, and how they’re trying to erase that. My in-laws weren’t just arguing with me like a family disagreement. They were using the altar and the parish committee, these respectable places, to push me out and make everyone forget my place.\n\nSo yes, when people talk about the situation, I don’t believe it’s random. I think there is usually something specific underneath it, something about me, something they want people to believe about me without saying it plainly. That’s how they get away with it.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you think could ever make you doubt your belief that your in-laws are plotting against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know what could make me doubt it, because to me it isn’t just a feeling that came out of nowhere. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t simply having a disagreement with me or being unpleasant at a family gathering. That would be one thing. Families argue, people get proud, people say things. But this was more than that.\n\nThey were using the altar, using the parish committee, using those positions where everyone is supposed to be respectful and proper, to move me aside. And it wasn’t only about one seat or one task or one conversation. It was about making people forget what I have done for this family, making it seem like I don’t matter, like my years of helping and organizing and being there can just be wiped away.\n\nSo when you ask if anything could make me doubt it… I suppose if I saw them stop, truly stop, and acknowledge what they’ve done, maybe that would make me look at it differently. But just someone telling me, “No, no, you misunderstood,” that doesn’t touch what I’ve seen. Because I know the difference between an argument and a plan to push someone out. And this was not just an argument.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your in-laws' actions against you are based on a reason that is not understandable to most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think to most people it probably doesn’t make sense, because they don’t see the whole thing. They just see little pieces and then they say, “Oh, that’s just a disagreement,” or, “That’s just how committees work,” or, “Maybe they didn’t mean anything by it.” But I came in because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me. It wasn’t like one harsh word at dinner or some ordinary family tension.\n\nThey were using the altar, and the parish committee, and all those little official church decisions to push me out. That’s the part people don’t understand, because it looks respectable from the outside. It looks like procedure. But when you’re the one being moved aside, when you’re the one suddenly not acknowledged, when everything you’ve done for the family is treated like it can be forgotten, you know there’s a reason behind it.\n\nSo yes, maybe the reason isn’t understandable to most people, because most people don’t want to believe someone would use something sacred or proper that way. But I understand it. They want me out of that place in the family, and they want everyone to forget what I’ve done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your in-laws' actions against you are so strange or unbelievable that no normal person would think that way?\n\nPatient: Strange to other people, maybe, yes. Unbelievable… I don’t know, because to me it is not unbelievable when you’ve lived through it and watched it happen piece by piece. But I understand that if I say it plainly — that my in-laws were not just arguing with me, that they were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family — some people look at me like, “Why would anyone do that?” or they think it sounds too much.\n\nBut that’s because they don’t see how it was done. It wasn’t someone standing up and announcing, “We are pushing her out.” It was little decisions, little comments, who gets included, who gets acknowledged, who is treated as if they belong there and who is made to feel like an outsider. And because it happened around the altar and through the parish committee, it looked respectable. That’s what makes it so hard. People see the church setting and they assume everything is proper.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it is so strange that no normal person would think it. I think a normal person would understand it if they saw the whole thing. But if they only hear one piece, then yes, maybe they think I’m making too much of it. I’m not. I came in because I knew this was not just a family argument. They were trying to erase my place.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I hear what you’re asking, and I’m trying to be fair about it. No matter how small? Maybe if there was some real acknowledgment, not just people telling me I’m too sensitive or that I misunderstood. If they actually admitted that they pushed me aside, that the way they used the altar and the parish committee was wrong, maybe I could reconsider parts of it.

But as it stands, it’s hard for me to doubt it, because I didn’t come here over one little argument. I came in because I knew my in-laws were not just disagreeing with me. They were using those church positions, those committee decisions, the respect people have around the altar, to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family.

So if you mean could one small comment from someone change my mind, no, not really. Because I’ve seen too much of it. It would take something real, something that showed they weren’t trying to erase my place. Otherwise it just feels like people want me to ignore what was right in front of me.

My in-laws, they keep acting like it’s just arguments or misunderstandings, but I know it’s more than that. They’ve been using the altar, and the parish committee too, to push me out, like to make me look like I don’t belong there anymore.\n\nAnd it’s not just about church, you know, it’s about my whole place in the family. After everything I’ve done for them, all the years, they’re trying to make everyone forget it. Like erase me. They smile and say certain things in public, but underneath it, they’re arranging things so people turn away from me. That’s why I came in. I need someone to understand that this isn’t just me being upset over an argument.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your in-laws are plotting against you in a way that's not obvious to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly it. It’s not obvious to people who aren’t watching closely, because they don’t come right out and say, “We’re pushing you out.” They do it through little things, through the altar, through who gets included, who gets spoken about, who gets left standing there like they don’t matter.\n\nAnd the parish committee… they’re involved too, or at least they’re being used. My in-laws know how important the church is, and they know if they can make me look like I don’t belong there anymore, then it’s like they’re taking away my place in the family too. After everything I’ve done for this family, they want people to forget it. They want it rewritten, like I was never the one who helped, who showed up, who carried things.\n\nSo yes, I do think they’re plotting, but it’s done in a way where if I explain it, people act like I’m making too much of it. But I know what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like your in-laws are plotting against you?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months now… maybe a little more in the way it’s felt, because I keep going over it in my head. But the real start was that fight with my in-laws over the Día de los Muertos altar. That’s when everything changed.\n\nBefore that, there were little tensions, sure, like families have, but after that altar argument it became this whole crisis. Since then, I’ve felt like they’ve been moving against me, using what happened there to make me look bad and push me aside. So yes… about two months, since that fight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your in-laws would actually be able to successfully erase you from the family and the church without you being able to stop them or others noticing?\n\nPatient: I think they could, yes… maybe not all at once where everyone would notice and say, “Oh, they’re erasing her,” but that’s the way it works. It’s little by little. They use the altar, they use the parish committee, they make it seem like I’m the difficult one or like I’m making trouble, and then people start stepping back from me without even realizing why.\n\nThat’s why I came in, because I knew this wasn’t just an argument with my in-laws. They weren’t just upset with me. They were using the altar and the church people to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. And if I don’t say something, if I don’t keep pointing it out, then yes, I think they could make it happen in a way where by the time anyone notices, my place is already gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your in-laws' actions against you are part of a larger conspiracy involving other people or organizations outside of your family and the church?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it some big outside conspiracy, like strangers somewhere I’ve never met are all planning it. I’m not saying that. But it doesn’t feel like it’s only inside the family either, because the church is part of it — the altar, the parish committee, the way people there are being pulled into it or used for it.\n\nThat’s why I came in, because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me over one thing. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make it look like I don’t belong, and to make people forget what I’ve done for this family. And maybe the committee doesn’t even understand the whole thing, maybe they think they’re just taking sides or keeping peace, but they’re still part of how it happens.\n\nSo outside the family and the church? I’m not sure. I haven’t seen it go beyond that. But within that circle, yes, it feels organized, like there’s a purpose to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that when other people at church or in your community talk about you or your family, they are always referring to your situation with your in-laws?\n\nPatient: Not always every single time, no… I don’t want to sound like I think every whisper in the church is about me. But when I hear people talking near me, or when they get quiet when I come around, I do think, “Is this about what happened? Did my in-laws say something again?” Because that’s how it started feeling after the altar situation.\n\nI came in because I knew this wasn’t just a normal family disagreement. My in-laws weren’t only arguing with me — they were using the Día de los Muertos altar, and the parish committee, to push me out. To make me look like I don’t belong there anymore, like I’m the problem, and to make everyone forget everything I’ve done for this family.\n\nSo when people at church talk about the family, or about the altar, or even just act different around me, it’s hard not to connect it back to that. I don’t think every conversation in the whole community is about me, but a lot of it feels tied to what my in-laws are doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your in-laws' actions against you are based on a reason that most people would not understand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Because if I try to explain it to someone who hasn’t seen the way they do things, it sounds like I’m just upset about a family argument, or like I’m being too sensitive about the altar. But it isn’t just that.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me. They were using the altar, and then the parish committee, to push me out little by little. That’s the part people don’t understand. They think, “Oh, it’s just a disagreement about church things,” or “families fight,” but it’s more than that. It’s about making me look like I don’t belong there, making people step away from me, making everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nAnd unless you’re the one being pushed out, you don’t see all the little signs. The comments, the way decisions get made without you, the way they act like they’re being respectful while they’re taking your place away. Most people would not understand that reason, no. They would just see the outside of it and think I’m making too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your in-laws' actions against you are based on a reason that is completely irrational or impossible?\n\nPatient: Completely irrational or impossible? No… I don’t think it’s impossible. That’s the thing. To me it feels very possible, because I saw how it was happening. It wasn’t like I woke up one day and made up some strange reason out of nowhere.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just having a normal argument with me. They were using the altar — and then the parish committee — to push me out. Not in a loud obvious way where everyone would say, “Oh, look what they’re doing,” but little by little, making it seem like I didn’t belong, like my part didn’t matter, like everything I’ve done for this family could just be erased.\n\nSo no, I don’t think the reason is irrational. Maybe other people don’t understand it, or they think I’m reading too much into it, but that doesn’t make it impossible. Families do things like that. People use church things, committees, respected places, to make themselves look right and make someone else look wrong. That’s what I believe was happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen or anyone who could tell you something that would make you change your mind about your in-laws plotting against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, people keep asking me that like there’s one thing someone could say and I would just drop it, but it isn’t that simple for me.\n\nIf my in-laws themselves came and admitted it, of course that would settle it, but they’re not going to do that. And if someone from the parish committee said, plainly, “No, nobody was trying to push you out, nobody was trying to make you look bad,” I would listen… but I would also wonder what they’re not saying, because they were part of how it happened. The altar was used, the committee was used, and I felt myself being moved aside while everyone acted like it was just normal church business.\n\nSo could I change my mind? Maybe if I saw real proof, not just people telling me to calm down or saying, “Families argue.” Because this was not just an argument. I came in because I knew my in-laws were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget everything I’ve done for this family. That’s not something I can just stop believing because someone tells me I misunderstood.\n\nI would need to see them actually stop, and I would need people to acknowledge what happened. Otherwise it feels like asking me to ignore what I saw with my own eyes.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that unrelated events or conversations are somehow connected to your situation with your in-laws?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, sometimes. Not every single thing, I’m not saying every conversation in the room is about me, but there were times when things that looked unrelated to other people felt connected to what my in-laws were doing.\n\nLike if something came up at the parish committee, or someone made a comment about the altar, or even if people suddenly got quiet when I walked near them, it didn’t feel separate. It felt like it was all part of the same thing — pushing me out, making me look like I didn’t belong there anymore. Other people might say, “Oh, that was just a meeting,” or “That person was just talking,” but when you are the one it’s happening to, you notice the pattern.\n\nThat is why I came in, really. Because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me like a normal family disagreement. They were using the altar and the parish committee, these respectable things, to push me out little by little and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. So yes, when I hear certain conversations or see certain decisions being made, I do connect them back to that. It’s hard not to, because it all fits together in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is anything your in-laws could do or say that would make you believe they are not plotting against you?\n\nPatient: I mean… they could say it, yes. They could sit there and say, “We’re not plotting against you, we’re not trying to push you out,” but I don’t know that just saying it would make me believe them. Because people can say anything when they know they’re being watched or questioned.\n\nWhat would matter to me is if their actions changed. If they stopped using the altar and the parish committee like that, stopped making decisions around me, stopped acting like my place in the family and in the church doesn’t count. Because that’s why I came in — I knew this wasn’t just some ordinary argument with my in-laws. It felt bigger than that. They were using those church things, the altar, the committee, to push me aside and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nSo if you’re asking if there is one sentence they could say that would make me think, “Oh, I was wrong,” then no, not really. I would need to see something real. I would need them to acknowledge what they did, not just deny it. Because if they only deny it, that just feels like more of the same — making me look like I’m the problem while they keep their hands clean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the plot against you is simple and straightforward or complex and involving many steps?\n\nPatient: It’s not simple. If it was simple, I think maybe people would understand me better, because then I could point to one thing and say, there, that’s what they did. But it’s not like that. It’s… it’s layered.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me, like a normal family disagreement. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, little by little, and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That takes steps. That takes people acting like everything is ordinary, like it’s just church business, when really it changes how others see me and where I’m allowed to stand.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s straightforward. It involves the family, the church, the committee, the way things are said and not said. It’s not just one conversation or one insult. It’s a whole arrangement, and that’s what makes it so hard to explain without sounding like I’m making too much of it. But I’m not. I know what I’ve seen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your in-laws' actions against you might be exaggerated or distorted in any way?\n\nPatient: Exaggerated? I… I know it may sound that way to you, because you’re hearing it from the outside. I understand that. If someone just says, “My in-laws are using church things against me,” people might think, “Well, maybe she’s taking it too personally.” I’m not unaware of how it sounds.\n\nBut to me, it doesn’t feel exaggerated. I came in because I knew this was not just my in-laws having a disagreement with me or being cold to me at family gatherings. It was more than that. They were using the altar, and the parish committee, these places where people are supposed to be respectful and fair, to push me out little by little. And not just push me out of a room — push me out of people’s minds, make everyone forget what I have done for this family.\n\nSo when you ask if it could be distorted, I don’t know how to answer that except to say I don’t think I’m making it up. Maybe I get upset when I talk about it, yes. Maybe I repeat myself because nobody seems to really hear the whole thing. But the pattern is there. It’s not just one comment or one decision. It’s the way they act, the way they use those church roles, the way suddenly my place doesn’t matter anymore.\n\nI suppose anything can look different depending on who is looking at it, but from where I’m standing, no, I don’t think I’m exaggerating. I think I’m finally saying out loud what has been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the plot against you is affecting your daily life in significant ways?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always been the one who could keep things going. The parish preparations, the festival things, the lists, who brings what, what needs to be cleaned, what needs to be set up — I used to be able to hold all of that in my head. And the house too. I had my way of doing things.\n\nBut lately I just… I couldn’t keep up. I would start something and then lose track of it, or I’d sit there thinking about what they were doing and how they were pushing me out, and the whole day would go. The festival tasks had to be taken over by my family because I wasn’t managing them the way I normally would. That was embarrassing, honestly, because those things mattered to me.\n\nAnd at home, it wasn’t just that the house was a little messy. I wasn’t keeping the routines. I was missing meals sometimes, forgetting what I was supposed to do next, not really taking care of myself the way I should. My husband started staying close to me because he could see I wasn’t reliable with those things. Not because I’m helpless, but because this has taken over so much space in my mind that ordinary things started slipping. So yes, it’s affecting my daily life a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any evidence or information that could be presented to you that would make you question or change your beliefs about your in-laws plotting against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know what evidence would change it, because the evidence is sort of already in how it happened. It wasn’t one thing where someone could hand me a paper and say, “See, this proves it,” or, “This disproves it.” That’s part of why it’s so hard to talk about.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me. If it were just an argument, I could accept that maybe tempers got high or people said things they didn’t mean. But this was them using the altar, using the parish committee, using those positions and those little decisions to move me aside. And then people act like it’s nothing, like it’s just normal church business, but it changes everything. It changes where I stand, what people remember, how they look at me, whether they acknowledge what I’ve done for this family.\n\nSo if you ask me, “Could someone show you something to make you question it?” I suppose if they all came forward honestly and admitted what they were doing, or if the committee stopped being used that way, maybe I could think differently. But just someone telling me, “No, that’s not happening,” wouldn’t be enough. Because I’ve already seen the pattern. I’ve seen the way I’m pushed out and then expected to smile like I don’t notice.\n\nI’m not trying to be stubborn. I know you’re asking whether I can consider another explanation. I can hear the question. But I don’t think this is a misunderstanding. I think people want me to doubt myself because then they don’t have to answer for what they’ve done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that when people talk about you or your situation, they are always referring to something specific that you've done or something about you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, a lot of the time it is about me, even when they don’t say my name directly. That’s what makes it so hard to explain, because people can pretend it’s just a general comment, or just church business, or just a committee decision. But I know the difference between something ordinary and something that is meant to point at me.\n\nWhen they talk about who should be involved, who should stand where, who should be recognized, who has “done enough” or who is “causing tension,” I hear that and I know it’s not just floating in the air. It goes back to what I’ve done, what I’ve tried to do for this family, and how they’re trying to erase that. My in-laws weren’t just arguing with me like a family disagreement. They were using the altar and the parish committee, these respectable places, to push me out and make everyone forget my place.\n\nSo yes, when people talk about the situation, I don’t believe it’s random. I think there is usually something specific underneath it, something about me, something they want people to believe about me without saying it plainly. That’s how they get away with it.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you think could ever make you doubt your belief that your in-laws are plotting against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know what could make me doubt it, because to me it isn’t just a feeling that came out of nowhere. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t simply having a disagreement with me or being unpleasant at a family gathering. That would be one thing. Families argue, people get proud, people say things. But this was more than that.\n\nThey were using the altar, using the parish committee, using those positions where everyone is supposed to be respectful and proper, to move me aside. And it wasn’t only about one seat or one task or one conversation. It was about making people forget what I have done for this family, making it seem like I don’t matter, like my years of helping and organizing and being there can just be wiped away.\n\nSo when you ask if anything could make me doubt it… I suppose if I saw them stop, truly stop, and acknowledge what they’ve done, maybe that would make me look at it differently. But just someone telling me, “No, no, you misunderstood,” that doesn’t touch what I’ve seen. Because I know the difference between an argument and a plan to push someone out. And this was not just an argument.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your in-laws' actions against you are based on a reason that is not understandable to most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think to most people it probably doesn’t make sense, because they don’t see the whole thing. They just see little pieces and then they say, “Oh, that’s just a disagreement,” or, “That’s just how committees work,” or, “Maybe they didn’t mean anything by it.” But I came in because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me. It wasn’t like one harsh word at dinner or some ordinary family tension.\n\nThey were using the altar, and the parish committee, and all those little official church decisions to push me out. That’s the part people don’t understand, because it looks respectable from the outside. It looks like procedure. But when you’re the one being moved aside, when you’re the one suddenly not acknowledged, when everything you’ve done for the family is treated like it can be forgotten, you know there’s a reason behind it.\n\nSo yes, maybe the reason isn’t understandable to most people, because most people don’t want to believe someone would use something sacred or proper that way. But I understand it. They want me out of that place in the family, and they want everyone to forget what I’ve done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your in-laws' actions against you are so strange or unbelievable that no normal person would think that way?\n\nPatient: Strange to other people, maybe, yes. Unbelievable… I don’t know, because to me it is not unbelievable when you’ve lived through it and watched it happen piece by piece. But I understand that if I say it plainly — that my in-laws were not just arguing with me, that they were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family — some people look at me like, “Why would anyone do that?” or they think it sounds too much.\n\nBut that’s because they don’t see how it was done. It wasn’t someone standing up and announcing, “We are pushing her out.” It was little decisions, little comments, who gets included, who gets acknowledged, who is treated as if they belong there and who is made to feel like an outsider. And because it happened around the altar and through the parish committee, it looked respectable. That’s what makes it so hard. People see the church setting and they assume everything is proper.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it is so strange that no normal person would think it. I think a normal person would understand it if they saw the whole thing. But if they only hear one piece, then yes, maybe they think I’m making too much of it. I’m not. I came in because I knew this was not just a family argument. They were trying to erase my place.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything, no matter how small, that could make you reconsider your belief that your in-laws are plotting against you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I hear what you’re asking, and I’m trying to be fair about it. No matter how small? Maybe if there was some real acknowledgment, not just people telling me I’m too sensitive or that I misunderstood. If they actually admitted that they pushed me aside, that the way they used the altar and the parish committee was wrong, maybe I could reconsider parts of it.\n\nBut as it stands, it’s hard for me to doubt it, because I didn’t come here over one little argument. I came in because I knew my in-laws were not just disagreeing with me. They were using those church positions, those committee decisions, the respect people have around the altar, to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family.\n\nSo if you mean could one small comment from someone change my mind, no, not really. Because I’ve seen too much of it. It would take something real, something that showed they weren’t trying to erase my place. Otherwise it just feels like people want me to ignore what was right in front of me."
